The authorization server MUST reject any JWT that does not contain its issuer identifier as its sole audience value." "In the absence of an application profile specifying otherwise, applications MUST compare the audience values using the Simple String Comparison method defined in Section 6.2.1 of RFC 3986 [RFC3986]." [§5](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/draft-ietf-oauth-rfc7523bis-11#section-5) replaces the last paragraph of [RFC 9126 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9126#section-2) for a pushed authorization request: "the issuer identifier URL of the authorization server according to [RFC8414] MUST be used as the sole value of the audience. The authorization server MUST reject any such JWT that does not contain its own issuer identifier as the sole audience value." Both sections add: "Client authentication JWTs SHOULD be explicitly typed by using the typ header parameter value client-authentication+jwt or another more specific explicit type value defined by a specification profiling this specification", and "it is NOT RECOMMENDED for servers to reject JWTs that do not have explicit types." [RFC 9126 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9126#section-2.1) has the authorization server "Authenticate the client in the same way as at the token endpoint (Section 2.3 of [RFC6749])." Of the client authentication parameters a pushed request carries, the same section says "they are relied upon only for client authentication and are not germane to the authorization request itself", so the pushed request the server stores holds neither the `client_assertion` nor the `client_assertion_type` value. [OpenID Connect Core §9](https://openid.net/specs/openid-connect-core-1_0.html#ClientAuthentication) says of the `jti`: "These tokens MUST only be used once, unless conditions for reuse were negotiated between the parties; any such negotiation is beyond the scope of this specification." and of the claim set: "Any Claims used that are not understood MUST be ignored." On the client, `ClientRegistration.ClientAssertionAudience` names the `aud` value at every endpoint the registration authenticates at: `ClientAssertionAudience.IssuerIdentifier` by default, `ClientAssertionAudience.TokenEndpoint` on offer. `ClientRegistration.IsClientAssertionExplicitlyTyped` (default `true`) selects the `typ` header parameter, `JWT` or `client-authentication+jwt`, through the `type` parameter of `ClientAssertionSigning.SignAsync`, and `ClientTokenEndpointAuthentication.AttachClientAssertionAsync` applies both choices to the assertion it attaches, so the code exchange, the refresh, the client credentials grant and a raw push present one shape. On the server, `PrivateKeyJwtClientAuthentication.BuildValidator` takes a `ClientAssertionAudiencePolicy`: the default `AuthorizationServerIdentities` accepts the issuer identifier and the request's token and pushed-authorization endpoint URLs, and `IssuerIdentifierOnly` is the opt-in that accepts the issuer identifier as the sole `aud` value, compared ordinally; combining `additionalAcceptedAudiences` with `IssuerIdentifierOnly` throws an `ArgumentException` when the validator is built. The validator reads no `typ`, so both `JWT` and `client-authentication+jwt` authenticate, reads the named claims only and ignores any other, and fails closed when the issuer identifier cannot be resolved. The application's turn: the registration's audience choice; the server's policy choice; the issuer identifier and the endpoint URLs the server accepts as the audience, which the application pins to configuration because a value derived from an unvalidated `Host` or forwarded header lets an assertion made for another authorization server match; `checkJtiReplayAsync` as the single-use mechanism, since a validator built without it leaves the single-use MUST to the application's own mechanism; and a `client_secret_jwt` validator, which the library does not ship, so the application applies the audience rule there itself. The library's own pushed-request sender attaches no client authentication; a confidential client's pushed request is authenticated by the application's push built on `ClientTokenEndpointAuthentication.AttachClientAssertionAsync`. Three deviations are named: the default audience policy, and two per-registration opt-ins. `ClientAssertionAudience.TokenEndpoint` is a deviation from §4 item b's MUST NOT, offered only for an authorization server that refuses its issuer identifier as the audience. The default `ClientAssertionAudiencePolicy.AuthorizationServerIdentities` is a deviation from §4 item b and §5's MUST reject a JWT without the issuer identifier as its sole audience value, taken because the text is an Internet-Draft and because of the asymmetry its §6 describes: "the resulting compatibility is not entirely symmetric", so a server enforcing the narrower rule refuses a client that follows RFC 7523 §3 item 3 or OpenID Connect Core §9. The default of `IsClientAssertionExplicitlyTyped` follows the §4 and §5 SHOULD, typing the assertion `client-authentication+jwt`; setting it to `false` writes the generic `JWT`, a deviation from that SHOULD, offered only for an authorization server that rejects `typ` values it does not know. Proven by `AuthCodeClientAuthenticationTests.CodeExchangeAssertionFollowsTheRegistrationsAudienceAndTypeAsync`, `AuthCodeClientAuthenticationTests.IssuerOnlyPolicyAcceptsOnlyTheIssuerAsSoleAudienceAsync`, `AuthCodeClientAuthenticationTests.PushedRequestAcceptsEachOfTheThreeRfc9126AudiencesWithoutExplicitWiring`, `ClientCredentialsGrantTests.ClientCredentialsAssertionNamesTheIssuerByDefaultAsync`, `ClientCredentialsGrantTests.IssuerOnlyPolicyHoldsAtTheTokenEndpointAsync`, `ClientAssertionSigningTests.TypeHeaderIsTheValueTheCallerNames`, `PrivateKeyJwtClientAuthenticationTests.IssuerOnlyPolicyWithAdditionalAudiencesIsRefusedAtBuild`, `PrivateKeyJwtClientAuthenticationTests.AssertionIsRefusedWhenIssuerResolutionFails`, `PrivateKeyJwtClientAuthenticationTests.IssuerOnlyPolicyCountsAnAudienceSequenceHeldAsAnIterator`, `ClientAssertionSigningTests.UnsetAssertionAudienceIsRefusedAsAnArgumentAndAddsNothingToTheForm`, `AuthCodeClientAuthenticationTests.ClaimSetsTheSpecificationsAllowAreAcceptedAtThePushedRequestAsync`, `AuthCodeClientAuthenticationTests.ClaimRuleViolationsAreRefusedAtThePushedRequestAsync`, `AuthCodeClientAuthenticationTests.FutureNotBeforeIsRefusedAtThePushedRequestAsync`, `AuthCodeClientAuthenticationTests.PushedRequestStoresNoClientAuthenticationParametersAsync`, and `RefreshConfidentialClientAuthenticationTests.RefreshAssertionNamesTheIssuerByDefaultAsync`.

- **Resource indicators: the grammar, the fold and the comparison key.** [RFC 8707 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2) says a `resource` value "MUST be an absolute URI, as specified by Section 4.3 of [RFC3986]. The URI MUST NOT include a fragment component.", provides the error code `invalid_target` for both request kinds ("The following error code is provided for an authorization server to indicate problems with the requested resource(s) in response to an authorization request or access token request.") and defines it ("The requested resource is invalid, missing, unknown, or malformed."). [§2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2.1) is the authorization request's section and directs: "If the authorization server fails to parse the provided value(s) or does not consider the resource(s) acceptable, it should reject the request with an error response using the error code "invalid_target" as the value of the "error" parameter and can provide additional information regarding the reasons for the error using the "error_description"." This library applies the same reading at the token endpoint as its own reading. `ResourceIndicator.Parse` reads a value's original spelling against [RFC 3986 Appendix A](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#appendix-A)'s `absolute-URI` and names the first part that fails as a `ResourceIndicatorFault`: every code point is US-ASCII ([RFC 3986 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-2): "The ABNF notation defines its terminal values to be non-negative integers (codepoints) based on the US-ASCII coded character set"), every `%` begins a two-digit triplet, a `#` is refused, and a value without a scheme is refused ([RFC 3986 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-3.1): "Scheme names consist of a sequence of characters beginning with a letter and followed by any combination of letters, digits, plus ("+"), period ("."), or hyphen ("-")."), so `:foo` and `://api.example.com/`, whose scheme is empty, are refused. For an `http` or `https` value `Parse` adds [RFC 9110 §4.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110#section-4.2.1) and [§4.2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110#section-4.2.2)'s "A recipient that processes such a URI reference MUST reject it as invalid." for an empty host (`HttpHost`) and the `"://" authority` production for a value without one (`HttpAuthority`), and [RFC 9110 §4.2.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110#section-4.2.4)'s "a recipient SHOULD parse for userinfo and treat its presence as an error; it is likely being used to obscure the authority for the sake of phishing attacks." for a `userinfo` (`HttpUserInfo`), so `https://api.example.com@other.example/` is refused instead of reading as one host while naming another. For every other scheme the generic syntax's `userinfo` is accepted, stored and minted into `aud` as sent, the deprecated `user:password` form included; the deviation is named below. [RFC 6749 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6749#section-3.1) is followed as written: "Parameters sent without a value MUST be treated as if they were omitted from the request." `ReadResourceOccurrences` drops an empty occurrence at every form ingress, the token exchange's included, so a request whose occurrences are all empty names no resource, while a whitespace-only occurrence is a value with no indicator in it and is malformed; a blank string inside a request object's `resource` claim is not a parameter sent without a value and is refused as malformed by the claim reader.
  - **The fold and the comparison key.** [RFC 8707 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2): "The authorization server may use the exact "resource" value as the audience or it may map from that value to a more general URI or abstract identifier for the given resource." The library maps by folding: the `ResourceIndicator.ComparisonKey` that `ResourceIndicator.Parse` derives for a value it accepts lowers the ASCII letters of the value's scheme and, when it has an authority, of its host outside percent triplets, following [RFC 3986 §6.2.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-6.2.2.1) ("the scheme and host are case-insensitive and therefore should be normalized to lowercase") and the [MCP authorization](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization#canonical-server-uri) sentence "implementations SHOULD accept uppercase scheme and host components for robustness and interoperability." The fold is culture-independent ASCII lowering; userinfo, port, path and query keep their spelling ("The other generic syntax components are assumed to be case-sensitive unless specifically defined otherwise by the scheme"). A value `Parse` refuses has no comparison key: `ReadResource` keeps such an occurrence unchanged, and `ResolveEffectiveResource` never matches a granted entry `Parse` refuses. `ResourceIndicator.ComparisonKey` is the fold of an accepted value, and two accepted indicators are equal exactly when their comparison keys are identical, which is [RFC 3986 §6.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-6.2.1)'s simple string comparison: "If two URIs, when considered as character strings, are identical, then it is safe to conclude that they are equivalent." `ResolveEffectiveResource` matches a token request's `resource` values against the granted set by `ComparisonKey` ([RFC 8707 §2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2.2): "the authorization server will issue an access token based on that subset of requested resources, whereas any refresh token that is returned is bound to the full original grant."): a requested value matches the granted entry of its identical spelling first and otherwise the first granted entry with its comparison key, and the issued `aud` carries each matched granted entry once, in the requested order and in the granted spelling. The granted set and the requested set are each deduplicated by exact spelling. Because the path is compared as sent, a multi-tenant resource that names its tenant in a path component keeps it ([RFC 8707 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-3): "it is important to use a specific resource URI including any portion of the URI that identifies the tenant, such as a path component.").
  - **The gates.** A `resource` value enters from the wire at four sites and each folds at its own site: `ReadResource` for the form field (the pushed request, the direct authorize request, and the token endpoint of the authorization-code, refresh and `client_credentials` grants), `AuthCodeRequestObjectExtensions.ReadResourceClaim` for the request object's claim (a JSON string or an array of strings, [RFC 8707 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2.1): "a single "resource" parameter value is represented as a JSON string while multiple values are represented as an array of strings"), the token exchange grant at its own site, which reads `ReadResourceOccurrences`, and the `resource` claim of a signed ID-JAG at the redeem, which `IdJagAssertionValidation`'s claim core reads. That claim is absent, a JSON string, or a non-empty JSON array of JSON strings, each value accepted by `ResourceIndicator.Parse` and surfaced folded, as its `ResourceIndicator.ComparisonKey`, in `IdJagAssertionValidationResult.Resource` in the order given ([draft-ietf-oauth-identity-assertion-authz-grant-04 §4.4.1](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/draft-ietf-oauth-identity-assertion-authz-grant-04#section-4.4.1): "If the resource claim is present, the Resource Authorization Server MUST process it according to Section 2 of [RFC8707]."); an empty string, an empty array, any other JSON type (a number, a boolean, an object, a JSON `null`), an element that is not a JSON string (a `null` element included) and a value the grammar refuses fail the validation as `IdJagValidationFailureReason.MalformedResource` before the `sub_id` step, and the application's `ValidateJwtBearerAssertionDelegate` is to return no grant for a failed result, so the token endpoint answers `invalid_grant` ([RFC 7523 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7523#section-3.1): "If the JWT is not valid, or the current time is not within the token's valid time window for use, the authorization server constructs an error response as defined in OAuth 2.0 [RFC6749]. The value of the "error" parameter MUST be the "invalid_grant" error code."). Answering `invalid_grant` there, and not `invalid_target`, is this library's reading, named with its reason in the deviations below; so is refusing the empty string and the empty array. The fold reaches the `resource` claim only: the grant's own `aud` is compared as signed, ordinally, so an `aud` that differs from the server's issuer only in the case of its scheme or host is `IdJagValidationFailureReason.AudienceMismatch` ([RFC 7523 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7523#section-3) item 3: "In the absence of an application profile specifying otherwise, compliant applications MUST compare the audience values using the Simple String Comparison method defined in Section 6.2.1 of RFC 3986 [RFC3986]."). At the form, request-object and token-exchange sites, `ValidateResourceOccurrences` checks every occurrence as received at the five form ingresses (the pushed request, the direct authorization request, the code and refresh redemptions in each step endpoint's pre-correlation step, so the answer is byte-identical for a grant that does not exist and one that does, and the `client_credentials` grant); `ValidateResourceIndicatorsShape` checks the request object's claim at the pushed and the by-value request object; the token exchange checks its own occurrences in its own request parsing. The refusal is `invalid_target` with one fixed `error_description`, "The resource parameter must be an absolute URI (RFC 3986 section 4.3) without a fragment.", never the value, and the request's trace carries the event `oauth.resource.malformed` with the tag `oauth.resource.fault`, never the value: at every form ingress the tag names the first failing part of the value, and a request object's `resource` claim that is not a JSON string or an array of strings, or holds a blank, non-string or whitespace-bearing element, is recorded as `Fragment`, the fault of the placeholder the claim reader hands the gate in its place (any other refused element is recorded by its own first failing part). A token request naming a resource outside the granted set is refused `invalid_target` with one fixed `error_description`, "The resource parameter must be a subset of the resource(s) originally granted." (or "The resource parameter must be a subset of the resource(s) originally granted; none were granted." when the grant carries none), and the refresh grant's `invalid_scope` refusal of a scope token that was not granted answers one fixed `error_description` the same way, "The scope parameter must be a subset of the scope originally granted."; neither names a requested value. The token exchange's `audience` is read occurrence by occurrence and never folded: an empty occurrence is dropped ([RFC 6749 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6749#section-3.1)) and a whitespace-only one is refused `invalid_request` ([RFC 8693 §2.2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8693#section-2.2.2): "The value of the "error" parameter MUST be the "invalid_request" error code."). The description sits inside [RFC 6749 §5.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6749#section-5.2)'s set ("Values for the "error_description" parameter MUST NOT include characters outside the set %x20-21 / %x23-5B / %x5D-7E."), and the authorize error redirect, in both its plain and its JARM branch, reduces every description it carries, an application's denial description included, to that set ([§4.1.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6749#section-4.1.2.1) states the same rule).
  - **The carry of an exchange's audience.** A refresh token the token exchange issues stores the `audience` values the authorization seam authorized as its granted set, in the slot a resource indicator is stored in, under the carry rule of `IsCarriedAudienceEntry` and not the grammar of an incoming `resource`: every entry holds no white space and parses as an absolute URI with no fragment component, so an entry the grammar refuses (a `userinfo` in an `https` value, a host that is not ASCII) is carried, as the seam spelled it and never folded. A refresh token is issued only for a grant whose audience a refresh of it issues again. An authorized audience entry that is null, empty or white space only is the seam's own defect: the exchange is answered 500 `server_error` with the fixed description "The server is not ready to serve this request." before the carry check, with nothing obtained or stored. An exchange whose authorization seam authorizes a refresh token for an audience holding an entry of another form that fails the rule is refused at the mint, before any identifier is obtained or any state is saved, with 400 `invalid_target` and the fixed description "A refresh token cannot be issued for the authorized audience.", which carries no value of the audience; [RFC 8693 §2.2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8693#section-2.2.2): "If the authorization server is unwilling or unable to issue a token for any target service indicated by the "resource" or "audience" parameters, the "invalid_target" error code SHOULD be used in the error response." The rule is this library's. The authorized audience is copied once by enumeration into the library's own buffer, and both refusals and the carry read that buffer, so the refresh state holds what was checked. The copy is one enumeration that adds each entry, never a bulk copy: a list that is a collection is read through its enumerator, never through `Count` and `CopyTo`, so the library's buffer is never handed to the application. An exchange whose seam names no audience is issued its refresh token, and a refresh of it is issued the producer's default. Proven by `TokenExchangeGrantTests.AnExchangeForARefreshTokenUnderAnAudienceHoldingAnEntryThatNamesNoTargetIssuesNothingAsync` (a null entry, an entry of white space beside an absolute URI: 500 with the exact body, no refresh state stored and no identifier drawn), `AnExchangeForARefreshTokenUnderAnAudienceARefreshCannotIssueIsRefusedAsync` (a logical name, a logical name with spaces, a URI-shaped name with a space, an absolute URI beside a logical name: 400 with the exact body and no refresh state stored), `AnExchangeForARefreshTokenUnderAnAudienceThatReadsDifferentlyAgainIsIssuedTheAudienceReadOnceAsync` (a list whose first enumeration answers an absolute URI and every later reading a logical name: the list is enumerated once at the mint and never read through its indexer, and the refresh state and the refresh's `aud` hold that first reading), `AnExchangeForARefreshTokenUnderAnAudienceCollectionIsIssuedTheEnumeratedAudienceWithoutABulkCopyAsync` (a collection whose enumerator yields an absolute URI and whose `CopyTo` writes a logical name: the refresh state and the refresh's `aud` hold the enumerator's entry, and `CopyTo` is never called) and `AnExchangeForARefreshTokenUnderAbsoluteUriAudiencesIssuesARefreshOfExactlyThoseAsync` (two absolute URIs, issued again as the refresh's `aud`) and `AnExchangeForARefreshTokenUnderNoAudienceIssuesARefreshOfTheDefaultAudienceAsync` (no audience, the refresh's `aud` the issuer). [RFC 8693 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8693#section-2.1) defines `audience` as "The logical name of the target service where the client intends to use the requested security token", so the granted set is deduplicated by exact spelling and not by comparison key: a resource indicator is folded before it is stored, so its exact spelling and its comparison key agree, while two carried audiences that differ only in the case of the scheme or the host are two names, both issued by a refresh that names no `resource`; a request's `resource` is folded at its ingress (`ReadResource`), so a refresh naming either spelling arrives as the folded one and is answered with the carried audience stored in the folded spelling when there is one and otherwise with the first carried audience with its comparison key, and the carried audience stored in the other spelling is issued only by a refresh that names no `resource`.
  - **The access token's audience.** [RFC 9068 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9068#section-3): "If the request includes a "resource" parameter (as defined in [RFC8707]), the resulting JWT access token "aud" claim SHOULD have the same value as the "resource" parameter in the request." The issued `aud` carries the granted entry's stored spelling, which is the folded one (the comparison above), so a request that spells its `resource` with capitals in the scheme or host receives an `aud` in another spelling than its own: a deviation from that SHOULD, named below, which follows from the mapping [RFC 8707 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2) permits. "If the request does not include a "resource" parameter, the authorization server MUST use a default resource indicator in the "aud" claim. If a "scope" parameter is present in the request, the authorization server SHOULD use it to infer the value of the default resource indicator to be used in the "aud" claim." A request that names no `resource` takes its default from the application's audience resolver, `ResolveAccessTokenAudienceDelegate` (`AuthorizationServerIntegration.ResolveAccessTokenAudienceAsync`), whose library default `Rfc9068AccessTokenProducer.DefaultResolveAccessTokenAudienceAsync` reads `ClientRecord.ScopeToAudience` for the granted scopes; when the resolver answers none, the default resource indicator is the issuer, the value the token's `iss` takes, so every access token carries `aud`. "If the values in the "scope" parameter refer to different default resource indicator values, the authorization server SHOULD reject the request with "invalid_scope" as described in Section 4.1.2.1 of [RFC6749]." The default resolver answers the union of the granted scopes' audiences instead, a deviation of the default named below; an application that wants the refusal supplies its own resolver. "The authorization server MUST NOT issue a JWT access token if the authorization granted by the token would be ambiguous." and [§5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9068#section-5)'s "The details on how to recognize and mitigate this and other ambiguous situations is highly scenario dependent and hence is out of scope for this profile." are the application's: its resolver, its `ScopeToAudience` and its authorization seams decide which scopes and audiences one token carries.
  - **The application's turn.** The library owns the syntax, the fold and the key; the policy is the application's. [RFC 8707 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2.1): "the authorization server MAY process the request with no specific resource or by using a predefined default resource value. Alternatively, the authorization server MAY require clients to specify the resource(s) they intend to access and MAY fail requests that omit the parameter with an "invalid_target" error." [§2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2.2): "To the extent possible, when issuing access tokens, the authorization server should downscope the scope value associated with an access token to the value the respective resource is able to process and needs to know." [§3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-3): "an authorization server may be unwilling or unable to fulfill a token request with multiple resources." [RFC 8693 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8693#section-2.1): "audience" values "must be unique within that server to ensure that they are properly interpreted as the intended type of value." [RFC 9110 §4.2.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110#section-4.2.3): "distinct resources SHOULD NOT be identified by HTTP URIs that are equivalent after normalization (using any method defined in Section 6.2 of [URI])." A default or required resource, the downscoping, the refusal of several resources, the uniqueness of audience values and any identity between two indicators beyond the fold are decisions of the application's seams: `EvaluateAuthorizationRequestDelegate` receives the folded values as `AuthorizationRequestEvaluation.RequestedResource` and answers `AuthorizationDenialReason.InvalidTarget`, which the library maps to `invalid_target`; `AuthorizeTokenExchangeAsync` receives the folded `resource` values (an `audience` value is a logical name and reaches it as sent) and a denial naming an audience answers `invalid_target`.
  - **The client's turn.** The library's request builders carry the application's `resource` value to the wire without folding it: `TokenExchangeRequestBuilder` refuses an entry that the shared resource-indicator grammar refuses and sends every other entry as its own occurrence, unfolded, and the request-object start options refuse an entry holding white space, and nothing else is checked. The client-side clauses are the application's: the [MCP authorization](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization#resource-parameter-implementation) `resource` parameter "MUST be included in both authorization requests and token requests", "MUST identify the MCP server that the client intends to use the token with" and "MUST use the canonical URI of the MCP server"; [RFC 8707 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2) "The client SHOULD provide the most specific URI that it can for the complete API or set of resources it intends to access." and "The client SHOULD use the base URI of the API as the "resource" parameter value unless specific knowledge of the resource dictates otherwise."; [RFC 8707 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-3) "Whenever feasible, the "resource" parameter should correspond to the network-addressable location of the protected resource." and, for an abstract identifier, "it is the client's responsibility to validate out of band that any network endpoint to which tokens are sent are the intended audience for that identifier."; [RFC 9110 §4.2.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110#section-4.2.4) "A sender MUST NOT generate the userinfo subcomponent (and its "@" delimiter) when an "http" or "https" URI reference is generated within a message as a target URI or field value."; and the sender MUST NOTs of [§4.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110#section-4.2.1) and [§4.2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110#section-4.2.2) against an empty host. The application owns the canonical form it sends.
  - **Deviations.** Each is named with its reason.
    - The token exchange answers a malformed `resource` with `invalid_target`, a deviation from [RFC 8693 §2.2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8693#section-2.2.2)'s "If the request itself is not valid" and "The value of the "error" parameter MUST be the "invalid_request" error code." The reason is [RFC 8707 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2), which provides the error code for an access token request ("The following error code is provided for an authorization server to indicate problems with the requested resource(s) in response to an authorization request or access token request."), together with the authorization request's instruction of [RFC 8707 §2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2.1) for a value the server fails to parse, which this library applies at the token endpoint as its own reading, and RFC 8693 Section 2.2.2's "the "invalid_target" error code SHOULD be used in the error response" for a target service the server is unable to issue for: this library reads the two as one error code per parameter at every endpoint, the token exchange included.
    - Repeated `resource` parameters are aggregated, a deviation from [RFC 6749 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6749#section-3.1)'s "Request and response parameters MUST NOT be included more than once." The reason is that [RFC 8707 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2) is the rule for this parameter and supersedes the general one: "Multiple "resource" parameters MAY be used to indicate that the requested token is intended to be used at multiple resources."
    - A `resource` carrying a query component is accepted, a deviation from [RFC 8707 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2)'s "It SHOULD NOT include a query component". The clause itself recognizes "that there are cases that make a query component a useful and necessary part of the resource parameter, such as when one or more query parameters are used to scope requests to an application", and the library accepts the value the client names.
    - Only the scheme and the host are normalized, a deviation from the further normalizations [RFC 3986 §6.2.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-6.2.2.1) ("the hexadecimal digits within a percent-encoding triplet (e.g., "%3a" versus "%3A") are case-insensitive and therefore should be normalized to use uppercase letters for the digits A-F"), [§6.2.2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-6.2.2.2) ("These URIs should be normalized by decoding any percent-encoded octet that corresponds to an unreserved character"), [§6.2.2.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-6.2.2.3) ("URI normalizers should remove dot-segments by applying the remove_dot_segments algorithm to the path") and [§6.2.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-6.2.3) ("a URI that uses the generic syntax for authority with an empty path should be normalized to a path of "/"" and an explicit port "for which the port is empty or the default for the scheme" "should be removed") describe, that [RFC 9110 §4.2.3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110#section-4.2.3) repeats for `http` and `https` (omit the default port, provide a path of "/", "Characters other than those in the "reserved" set are equivalent to their percent-encoded octets"), and that the [MCP authorization](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization#canonical-server-uri) note asks for ("implementations SHOULD consistently use the form without the trailing slash"). None is applied and every other spelling is compared exactly and refused when it differs, so `https://x` and `https://x/`, `%2f` and `%2F`, `:443` and no port, and `/a/../b` and `/b` are different indicators, and a token request naming one spelling of a granted pair is refused `invalid_target` as not granted (`NarrowingRefusesAnEquivalentSpellingTheLibraryDoesNotNormalizeAsync`). The reason is fail closed: an equivalent spelling is refused, never widened into a grant or an audience the resource owner did not name, and [RFC 3986 §6.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-6.2.1) states that identical strings are equivalent.
    - For every scheme other than `http` and `https`, the `userinfo` the generic syntax admits is accepted, stored and minted into `aud` as sent, the deprecated `user:password` form included: a deviation from [RFC 3986 §3.2.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-3.2.1)'s "should reject the storage of such data in unencrypted form". The reason is that the generic grammar admits it and no rule of RFC 8707 forbids it. [§7.6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3986#section-7.6) names the look-alike hazard, a `userinfo` "appearing to identify one (trusted) naming authority while actually identifying a different authority hidden behind the noise", which the library refuses where [RFC 9110 §4.2.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110#section-4.2.4) says to (`http` and `https`) and leaves to the application's seams for every other scheme.
    - The choice [RFC 8707 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2) leaves ("may use the exact "resource" value as the audience or it may map from that value") is taken as a mapping: the fold is applied before storage, so a stored grant holds the folded spelling. This is a permitted choice and not a departure.
    - A stored grant value the grammar refuses (an IRI, or any other value outside the ASCII grammar, such as a carried audience the grammar refuses) is compared as itself by `ComparisonKey` and applies only to a token request that names no resource, so it can never share a comparison key with a value the request supplies.
    - A malformed `resource` claim of a signed ID-JAG is answered `invalid_grant` ([RFC 7523 §3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7523#section-3.1)), not the `invalid_target` that [RFC 8707 §2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8707#section-2) registers for a resource that is "invalid, missing, unknown, or malformed", although [draft-ietf-oauth-identity-assertion-authz-grant-04 §4.4.1](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/draft-ietf-oauth-identity-assertion-authz-grant-04#section-4.4.1) processes the claim "according to Section 2 of [RFC8707]". The reason: the claim is part of a signed grant, so a grant carrying a malformed one is a JWT that is not valid ("If the JWT is not valid, or the current time is not within the token's valid time window for use, the authorization server constructs an error response as defined in OAuth 2.0 [RFC6749]. The value of the "error" parameter MUST be the "invalid_grant" error code."), not a request parameter RFC 8707 Section 2 answers with `invalid_target` (`IdJagValidationFailureReason.MalformedResource`).
    - An ID-JAG `resource` claim that is the empty string or the empty array is refused as `MalformedResource`. [Draft-04 §3.1](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/draft-ietf-oauth-identity-assertion-authz-grant-04#section-3.1) names the claim's shapes, "(either a single URI or an array of URIs)", and no sentence of Section 3.1 or Section 4.4.1 refuses an array of none or an empty string; the refusal is this library's fail-closed reading. The reason: such a claim names no resource server, and read as absent it would let the grant reach the default audience the issuer did not name (`IdJagValidationFailureReason.MalformedResource`).
    - The access token's `aud` carries the granted entry's folded spelling where the request named another spelling of the same indicator, a deviation from [RFC 9068 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9068#section-3)'s "the resulting JWT access token "aud" claim SHOULD have the same value as the "resource" parameter in the request". The reason: the fold is applied before storage (the permitted mapping above), and one spelling per resource server is what a resource server compares its audience against.
    - The default audience resolver answers the union of the audiences the granted scopes map to, where [RFC 9068 §3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9068#section-3) reads "If the values in the "scope" parameter refer to different default resource indicator values, the authorization server SHOULD reject the request with "invalid_scope" as described in Section 4.1.2.1 of [RFC6749]." The reason: which scopes may share one token is the application's policy; an application that wants the refusal supplies its own `ResolveAccessTokenAudienceDelegate`.
  - Proven by `Rfc8707ResourceIndicatorTests`: the grammar and its refusals at the pushed request, `PushedAuthorizationRequestRejectsNonAbsoluteResourceWithInvalidTarget`, `ftp://user@host/` accepted), `ParseReadsEveryIpLiteralForm`, `EqualityAndHashingUseOnlyTheComparisonKey` and `EqualityKeepsEveryDifferenceOutsideTheFold` (equality is the comparison key's), and one row for each fault, whose refusal carries no accepted value: `ParseRefusesEmpty`, `ParseRefusesNonAscii`, `ParseRefusesScheme`, `ParseRefusesAuthority`, `ParseRefusesPath`, `ParseRefusesQuery`, `AudienceTrailingSlashIsInvalidToken` and `AudienceDefaultPortIsInvalidToken`. `RefreshGrantTests`, the scope refusal's fixed description:
